where the plus holds for timelike and the minus for spacelike congruences. This means that for any
V^{a},W^{b}\in H_{p}
,
P_{ab}
acts as the metric since
P_{ab}V^{a}W^{b}=g_{ab}V^{a}W^{b}
, while any vector parallel to
l^{a}
is annihilated. For a null congruence, the choice of
P_{ab}
is less natural since
H_{p}
is of codimension two. We therefore choose another null vector
k^{a}
which has
k_{a}l^{a}=-1
and is parallel transported, and define

\displaystyle P_{ab}=g_{ab}+l_{a}k_{b}+k_{a}l_{b}\ ;

(A.20)

again, we notice that for
V^{a},W^{b}\in H_{p}
,
P_{ab}V^{a}W^{b}=V_{a}W^{a}
while vectors that are parallel to either
l^{a}
or
k^{a}
are annihilated. With (
A.19
) and (
A.20
), we note that
g^{ab}P_{ab}=d-n
, which we use to express the expansion, shear, and vorticity in terms of
\nabla_{a}l_{b}
:

\displaystyle\theta=

\displaystyle\ \nabla_{a}l^{a}\ ,

(A.21)

\displaystyle\sigma_{ab}=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{2}\left(\nabla_{a}l_{b}+\nabla_{b}l_{a}\right)-\frac{\nabla_{c}l^{c}}{d-n}P_{ab}\ ,

(A.22)

\displaystyle\omega_{ab}=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{2}\left(\nabla_{a}l_{b}-\nabla_{b}l_{a}\right)\ .

(A.23)

Note that
\sigma_{ab}l^{a}=\omega_{ab}l^{a}=0
, and that for a null congruence
\sigma^{ab}k_{b}
and
\omega^{ab}k_{b}
are perpendicular to both
l^{a}
and
k^{a}
. Hence,
\sigma_{ab}
and
\omega_{ab}
are ‘spatial’ in the sense that there exists a coordinate system in which they have no temporal components; it follows that
\sigma_{ab}\sigma^{ab}\geq 0
and
\omega_{ab}\omega^{ab}\geq 0
. Much of the content of
\nabla_{a}l_{b}
is summarised by
\theta
, which measures the change in the volume of a
d-n
dimensional sphere of test particles travelling along the geodesic (besides
[
2
]
,
[
30
]
discusses this interpretation). Its evolution is described by the Raychaudhuri equation, which we derive by considering the derivative of
\theta
along the congruence:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}=l^{a}\nabla_{a}(\nabla_{b}l^{b})=

\displaystyle\ l^{a}\nabla_{b}\nabla_{a}l^{b}+l^{a}R^{b}_{\ \,cab}l^{c}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \nabla_{b}(l^{a}\nabla_{a}l^{b})-\left(\nabla_{b}l_{a}\right)(\nabla^{a}l^{b})-R^{b}_{\ \,cba}l^{a}l^{c}\ .

(A.24)

In the first line, we used (
A.14
). Since the geodesics in the congruence are affinely parametrised, the first term in (
A.24
) vanishes, and we can substitute (
A.18
) and
R_{ab}=R^{c}_{\ \,acb}
to find

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}=

\displaystyle\ -\frac{\theta^{2}}{(d-n)^{2}}P_{ba}P^{ab}-\sigma_{ba}\sigma^{ab}-\omega_{ba}\omega^{ab}-\frac{\theta}{d-n}(P_{ba}\sigma^{ab}+\sigma_{ba}P^{ab})-R_{ab}l^{a}l^{b}\ .

(A.25)

Using (
A.19
) and (
A.20
), we find
P_{ab}P^{ab}=d-n
, and combining them with (
A.22
) shows that
P_{ab}\sigma^{ab}=0
, so that we obtain the final form of the Raychaudhuri equation:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}=

\displaystyle\ -\frac{\theta^{2}}{d-n}-\sigma_{ab}\sigma^{ab}+\omega_{ab}\omega^{ab}-R_{ab}l^{a}l^{b}\ .

(A.26)

A slight simplification of this equation can be found by realising that if
\theta
measures changes in
d-n
dimensional volumes, there should be a length scale
\eta
, also known as a Jacobi field, associated with these volumes. In general, we define the Jacobi field as

\displaystyle\theta=\frac{1}{\eta^{d-n}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta^{d-n}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}=\frac{d-n}{\eta}\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\ ,

(A.27)

which allows for a straightforward interpretation of
\theta
as the relative change in a volume
\eta^{d-n}
. Furthermore, points where neighbouring geodesics intersect are now characterised by
\eta\rightarrow 0
; we refer to such points as caustics. In terms of
\eta
, the Raychaudhuri equation reads

\displaystyle\frac{d-n}{\eta}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}\eta}{\mathrm{d}\lambda^{2}}=-\sigma_{ab}\sigma^{ab}+\omega_{ab}\omega^{ab}-R_{ab}l^{a}l^{b}\ .

(A.28)

Physically, one can interpret
\sigma_{ab}
as describing the influence of distant inhomogeneities on a geodesic (describing e.g. gravitational lensing), so
\sigma_{ab}=0
only in rather special spacetimes
[
70
]
. On the other hand, it is quite straightforward to find an (initially) irrotational congruence, in which
\omega_{ab}=0
. This can be seen by constructing Gaussian normal coordinates, in which one can explicitly calculate
\omega_{ab}=0

[
9
,
30
]
; we will discuss this construction for null congruences (following
[
30
]
), since timelike congruences demonstrate similar behaviour.

Consider a spacetime
M
, and a codimension 2 spacelike submanifold
W\subset M
(i.e. a
d-2
dimensional submanifold with a timelike normal vector). Since
W
is spacelike, one of its normal vectors is timelike; the other one must be spacelike, because
g_{ab}
has Lorentzian signature. If
W
is orientable, the normal vectors define four orientations for null congruences emanating orthogonally from
W
: past- and future-directed, and propagating along or opposite to the spacelike normal vector
1
1

1

As
[
9
]
points out, the null geodesics emanating orthogonally from
W
do not form congruences, as they don’t cover an open region of spacetime. Instead they form null hypersurfaces (codimension 1 submanifolds with a null normal vector), each of which can be made into a congruence by deforming
W
in a direction not included in the respective hypersurface and emanating null geodesics from the deformed
W
with the same spatio-temporal orientation as the geodesics covering the hypersurface. We proceed assuming this has been done.
. Without loss of generality, we consider the congruence of future-directed null geodesics propagating along the spacelike normal vector of
W
and define
N\subset M
as the codimension 1 submanifold covered by this congruence. We can equip (a subset of)
W
with coordinates
x^{I}
(with
I\in\left\{2,\ldots,d-1\right\}
), and choose an affine parameter
u
for the null geodesics covering
N
such that
u
vanishes at
W
and increases towards the future of
W
; this uniquely defines
u
up to multiplication by a positive function of
x^{I}
. By declaring
x^{I}
to be constant along the geodesics, we define a coordinate system on
N
which is non-degenerate until the geodesics forming
N
intersect, i.e. until they encounter a caustic.

To extend these coordinates to a
d
dimensional region in
M
, we follow
[
30
]
by choosing a codimension 1 spacelike submanifold
S\subset M
with
W\subset S
. We then define a function
v:S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
S
is the union of codimension 2 spacelike submanifolds
W_{\alpha}=\left\{p\in S:v(p)=\alpha,\alpha\in K\subseteq\mathbb{R}\right\}
, with
W=W_{0}
. If the orientation of each
W_{\alpha}
is smoothly connected to that of
W
, the procedure from the previous paragraph can be repeated: extend the
x^{I}
to functions on
S
to equip every
W_{\alpha}
with coordinates and choose an affine parameter
u
for the future-directed null geodesics emanating orthogonally from
W_{\alpha}
along the spacelike normal vector, with
u=0
on
W_{\alpha}
. By declaring
v
and
x^{I}
to be constant along these geodesics, we have constructed a coordinate system
(u,v,x^{I})
for at least the region close to
W
and
N
.

Let us now examine the metric components in these coordinates. One can set
g_{uu}=0
, since
u
is the affine parameter of a null geodesic with constant
v
and
x^{I}
. Every such null geodesic is orthogonal to some
W_{\alpha}
, so
g_{uI}=0
on all
W_{\alpha}
; it follows that
g_{uI}
only depends on
u
. However, a curve with constant
v
and
x^{I}
is by definition a null geodesic with affine parameter
u
, so according to (
A.10
)
\Gamma^{\mu}_{uu}=0
. By (
A.8
) this means that
\partial_{u}g_{u\mu}=0
; hence
g_{uI}=0
identically.
g_{uv}
on the other hand cannot vanish (otherwise
g_{ab}
would be degenerate), so we define
g_{uv}=-e^{q}
, where
q=q(v,x^{I})
and the sign is chosen such that
u-\beta v=x^{I}=0
defines a timelike curve for sufficiently big
\beta>0
. Thus, in Gaussian normal coordinates the metric is

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}s^{2}=-e^{q}\left(\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v+\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}u\right)+g_{vv}\mathrm{d}v^{2}+g_{IJ}\mathrm{d}x^{I}\mathrm{d}x^{J}+g_{vI}\left(\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}x^{I}+\mathrm{d}x^{I}\mathrm{d}v\right)\ ,

(A.29)

where
g_{vv}
,
g_{vI}
, and
g_{IJ}
can all be functions of all coordinates. To calculate the vorticity in these coordinates, we recall that every null geodesic covering
N
can be described by fixing
v
and
x^{I}
and choosing
u=\lambda
. Every such geodesic has a tangent vector with components
l^{\mu}=\delta^{\mu}_{u}
, which means that the components of the vorticity tensor are

\displaystyle\omega_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\nabla_{\mu}l_{\nu}-\nabla_{\nu}l_{\mu}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\nabla_{\mu}g_{\nu u}-\nabla_{\nu}g_{\mu u}\right)=0\ ,

(A.30)

where we made use of the metric compatibility of the covariant derivative. Since (
A.30
) is manifestly tensorial, we conclude that any null congruence emanating orthogonally from a codimension 2 submanifold has
\omega_{ab}=0
, at least up to a caustic.

A.4
Weyl transformations

To finish this appendix, we consider the effect of a Weyl transformation on the geometry and geodesics of a manifold. A Weyl transformation is a local rescaling of the metric tensor, so if a spacetime
\tilde{M}
has a metric
\tilde{g}_{ab}
, a Weyl transformation assigns it a new metric
g_{ab}
by mapping

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{ab}(x)\rightarrow g_{ab}(x)=\Omega^{2}(x)\tilde{g}_{ab}(x)\ ,

(A.31)

where
\Omega:\tilde{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a smooth, strictly positive function (since we assume that
g^{ab}
exists). Alternatively, one may consider two manifolds
\tilde{M}
and
M
which can (partially) be identified through a diffeomorphism. If, whenever this identification can be made, the metrics
\tilde{g}_{ab}
and
g_{ab}
of
\tilde{M}
and
M
respectively are related as in (
A.31
), then a Weyl transformation is a map from
\tilde{M}
to
M
. This allows us to interpret two types of points which warrant special attention, namely those for which
\Omega\rightarrow 0
and those for which
\Omega\rightarrow\infty
. The latter case corresponds to an edge of
M
because continuing
g_{ab}
beyond a divergence would mean that
g_{ab}
is no longer a smooth tensor field. Assuming that the Weyl transformation is invertible we see that
\Omega\rightarrow 0
corresponds to the edge of
\tilde{M}
by the same argument.

Given that
g_{ab}(x)=\Omega^{2}(x)\tilde{g}_{ab}(x)
, it is straightforward to calculate the transformed Levi-Civita connection in the coordinate basis:

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}g^{\rho\lambda}\left(\partial_{\mu}g_{\lambda\nu}+\partial_{\nu}g_{\lambda\mu}-\partial_{\lambda}\ g_{\mu\nu}\right)=\tilde{\Gamma}^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}+\Omega^{-1}\left(\delta^{\rho}_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\Omega+\delta^{\rho}_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}\Omega-\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}\tilde{g}^{\rho\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}\Omega\right)\ .

(A.32)

Following
[
74
]
, we recognise that the partial derivatives in the brackets can be replaced by covariant derivatives, allowing us to give the effect of a Weyl transformation on the covariant derivative of an arbitrary vector
X^{a}
in a manifestly coordinate-independent way:

\displaystyle\nabla_{b}X^{a}=\tilde{\nabla}_{b}X^{a}+\Omega^{-1}\left(\delta^{a}_{b}\tilde{\nabla}_{c}\Omega+\delta^{a}_{c}\tilde{\nabla}_{b}\Omega-\tilde{g}_{bc}\tilde{g}^{ad}\tilde{\nabla}_{d}\Omega\right)X^{c}\ .

(A.33)

Consider now a geodesic
\gamma
with tangent vector
\tilde{X}^{a}
and affine parameter
\tilde{\lambda}
in
\tilde{M}
. Due to the diffeomorphism between
\tilde{M}
and
M
,
\gamma
corresponds to a curve in
M
with the same tangent vector, but let us choose a different parameter
\lambda
, so that the tangent vector of
\gamma
in
M
is
X^{a}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\lambda}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\tilde{X}^{a}
. It is then again a matter of computation to find

\displaystyle X^{a}\nabla_{a}X^{b}=

\displaystyle\ \left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\lambda}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\right)^{2}\tilde{X}^{a}\tilde{\nabla}_{a}\tilde{X}^{b}+\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\left(\Omega^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\lambda}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\right)\Omega^{-2}\tilde{X}^{b}-\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\lambda}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\right)^{2}\tilde{g}_{cd}\tilde{X}^{c}\tilde{X}^{d}\Omega^{-1}\tilde{g}^{ab}\tilde{\nabla}_{a}\Omega\ .

(A.34)

The first term on the right-hand side will vanish due to the affine parameterisation of
\gamma
in
\tilde{M}
. The final term will, in general, not vanish if
\gamma
is time- or spacelike in
\tilde{M}
, meaning that
\gamma
will not be a geodesic in
M
in these cases. If however
\gamma
is a null geodesic in
\tilde{M}
then it will be a null geodesic in
M
as well, and
\lambda
an affine parameter if we choose

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\lambda}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}=\Omega^{-2}\ .

(A.35)

It may also be useful to know how the quantities describing a congruence transform. Since time- and spacelike geodesics are not mapped to geodesics, we consider a null congruence, with tangent vector field
l^{a}=\Omega^{-2}\tilde{l}^{a}
; if we choose the auxiliary vector fields
k^{a}
and
\tilde{k}^{a}
from the definition (
A.20
) of the projection tensor such that
k_{a}l^{a}=\tilde{k}_{a}\tilde{l}^{a}=-1
, it follows that
k^{a}=\tilde{k}^{a}
and
P_{ab}=\Omega^{2}\tilde{P}_{ab}
. By then applying (
A.33
) repeatedly to (
A.21
)-(
A.23
), we obtain

\displaystyle\theta=

\displaystyle\ \Omega^{-2}\tilde{\theta}+(d-2)\Omega^{-2}\frac{\mathrm{d}\ln\Omega}{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\lambda}}\ ,

(A.36)

\displaystyle\sigma_{ab}=

\displaystyle\ \tilde{\sigma}_{ab}-\tilde{l}_{a}\tilde{\nabla}_{b}\ln\Omega-\tilde{l}_{b}\tilde{\nabla}_{a}\ln\Omega-\left(\tilde{l}_{a}\tilde{k}_{b}+\tilde{l}_{b}\tilde{k}_{a}\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}\ln\Omega}{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\lambda}}\ ,

(A.37)

\displaystyle\omega_{ab}=

\displaystyle\ \tilde{\omega}_{ab}\ ,

(A.38)

where of course the indices of quantities with and without tildes are raised and lowered by
\tilde{g}_{ab}
and
g_{ab}
respectively. We conclude that while a Weyl transformation cannot introduce vorticity, it seemingly introduces a shear; however, note that
\sigma_{ab}\sigma^{ab}=\Omega^{-4}\tilde{\sigma}_{ab}\tilde{\sigma}^{ab}
. The transformation of the Jacobi field can be found by combining (
A.36
) with (
A.27
), leading to

\displaystyle\eta=\Omega\tilde{\eta}\ .

(A.39)

Finally, we can analyse the effect of a Weyl transformation on the curvature of the manifold. This can be done by using (
1.6
) to compute the components of the transformed Riemann tensor explicitly and write them in a coordinate independent way; since we do not need the full Riemann tensor, we only quote the result for the Ricci tensor and scalar here:

\displaystyle R_{ab}=

\displaystyle\ \tilde{R}_{ab}-(d-2)\left(\tilde{\nabla}_{a}\tilde{\nabla}_{b}\ln\Omega-\tilde{\nabla}_{a}\ln\Omega\tilde{\nabla}_{b}\ln\Omega\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\ \qquad\ \qquad\ \qquad\ \qquad\ -\tilde{g}^{cd}\left(\tilde{\nabla}_{c}\tilde{\nabla}_{d}\ln\Omega+(d-2)\tilde{\nabla}_{c}\ln\Omega\tilde{\nabla}_{d}\ln\Omega\right)\tilde{g}_{ab}\ ,

(A.40)

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle\ \Omega^{-2}\tilde{R}-(d-1)\Omega^{-2}\tilde{g}^{ab}\left(2\tilde{\nabla}_{a}\tilde{\nabla}_{b}\ln\Omega+(d-2)\tilde{\nabla}_{a}\ln\Omega\tilde{\nabla}_{b}\ln\Omega\right)\ .

(A.41)

Appendix B
Field theoretical background

This appendix will remind the reader of some basic aspects of quantum field theory (QFT). First, section
B.1
discusses the formalism of classical field theory. Section
B.2
then quantises these classical fields using canonical quantisation; section
B.3
presents an alternative way of representing calculations in QFT. This appendix concludes with a discussion of conformally invariant field theories (CFTs) in section
B.4
.

There is much literature on quantum field theory. Two texts that make use of canonical quantisation are
[
75
]
and
[
76
]
; our treatment is mostly based on the former.

B.1
Classical field theory

As explained in section
2.1
, physics is believed to obey the postulate of local causality: a causal influence can only propagate between two points
p
and
q
if there exists a curve with an everywhere timelike or null tangent vector between them
[
2
]
. Said differently, if a signal can affect the state of a physical system at some point
p
, then it must have travelled between its source and
p
at a speed not exceeding the speed of light. A natural way to encode this principle into natural laws is to use local fields. These act as the medium through which interactions propagate, and limit the speed of propagation by demanding that at every point, the field can only influence its immediate (causally connected) surroundings. Examples of fields in classical physics include the electromagnetic field and, in general relativity, the metric tensor.

To formulate the dynamics of a classical field in a diffeomorphism invariant way, we turn to the principle of least action, for which a quantity called the action
S
is required. In a non-relativistic context,
S
would be defined as
S=\int L\,\mathrm{d}t
, the time integral of a Lagrangian
L
; a diffeomorphism invariant generalisation defines
S
with an integral over spacetime, namely

\displaystyle S=\int\mathcal{L}\left(\Phi,\nabla_{a}\Phi,g_{ab}\right)\sqrt{-g}\,\mathrm{d}^{d}x\ .

(B.1)

Here,
\mathcal{L}
is the Lagrangian density (which we also call the Lagrangian), assumed to be a functional of the field
\Phi
and its first derivative
\nabla_{a}\Phi\,
, and
g
is the determinant of
(g_{\mu\nu})
; the factor
\sqrt{-g}
ensures that the volume element
\sqrt{-g}\,\mathrm{d}^{d}x
is diffeomorphism invariant.

According to the principle of least action, the configuration of
\Phi
will now be such that
S
is minimised. Such a minimum occurs at a stationary point of
S
, which is a field configuration such, that an infinitesimal variation in
\Phi
does not change
S
at first order in the variation (this is analogous to the way the first derivative of a function vanishes at an extremum). In general, a variation
\Phi\rightarrow\Phi+\delta\Phi
leads to a change in
S
of

\displaystyle\delta S=

\displaystyle\ \int\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\Phi}\delta\Phi+\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\nabla_{a}\Phi}\delta\nabla_{a}\Phi\right)\sqrt{-g}\,\mathrm{d}^{d}x+\mathcal{O}(\delta\Phi^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \int\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\Phi}-\nabla_{a}\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\nabla_{a}\Phi}\right)\right)\delta\Phi\sqrt{-g}\,\mathrm{d}^{d}x+\int\nabla_{a}\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\nabla_{a}\Phi}\delta\Phi\right)\sqrt{-g}\,\mathrm{d}^{d}x+\mathcal{O}(\delta\Phi^{2})\ ,

(B.2)

where we have assumed that
\delta\nabla_{a}\Phi=\nabla_{a}\delta\Phi
, integrated by parts, and used that
\nabla_{a}\sqrt{-g}=0
due to the metric compatibility of the covariant derivative. The second integral in (
B.2
) is a boundary term, which vanishes if we assume that
\delta\Phi\rightarrow 0
at the edge of spacetime. At a stationary point of
S
, we should have
\delta S=\mathcal{O}(\delta\Phi^{2})
for all
\delta\Phi
, which by (
B.2
) implies that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\Phi}-\nabla_{a}\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\nabla_{a}\Phi}\right)=0

(B.3)

at a stationary point of
S
. We therefore conclude that these are the equations of motion (or Euler-Lagrange equations) which a classical field obeys; if there are multiple fields, (
B.3
) holds for every one of them. Finally, we may define a conjugate momentum
\Pi(x)
to the field
\Phi(x)
; analogously to the non-relativistic definition
p=\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot{x}}
, we do this by defining

\displaystyle\Pi(x)=\frac{\partial\sqrt{-g}\mathcal{L}}{\partial(\nabla_{t}\Phi)}\ ,

(B.4)

where
t
is some timelike coordinate.

So far, we have not specified the field
\Phi
; it may be a scalar field, but the same equations apply if it is a vector or tensor field (a treatment of spinors in curved spacetime is beyond the scope of this thesis). Let us therefore consider two important examples of classical field theories: the non-minimally coupled but otherwise free real scalar field
\phi
and general relativity. The Lagrangian of the former is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{a}\phi\nabla^{a}\phi-\frac{1}{2}\left(m^{2}+\xi R\right)\phi^{2}\ ,

(B.5)

where
m
is the mass of the field,
R
the Ricci scalar, and
\xi
the non-minimal coupling parameter; the latter is named for the fact that
\xi\neq 0
connects
\phi
to the geometry of spacetime in a way besides the factor of
\sqrt{-g}
in (
B.1
). By (
B.3
), the equations of motion for this field are

\displaystyle\left(\nabla_{a}\nabla^{a}-(m^{2}+\xi R)\right)\phi=0\ .

(B.6)

This equation is rather difficult to solve in general. However, in many circumstances the geometry is adequately modelled by Minkowski spacetime, which has
R=0
and can be equipped with Cartesian coordinates
x^{\mu}=(t,\mathbf{x})
. (
B.6
) then reduces to a familiar form of the Klein-Gordon equation, namely
\left(\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\mu}-m^{2}\right)\phi=0
. To solve this equation, we may expand the dependence of
\phi
on
\mathbf{x}
in Fourier modes:

\displaystyle\phi(t,\mathbf{x})=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d-1}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{d-1}}\phi(t,\mathbf{p})e^{i\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\ ,

(B.7)

where
\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{x}=\delta_{ij}p^{i}x^{j}
with
\delta_{ij}
the Kronecker delta, and
\phi(t,\mathbf{p})=\phi^{*}(t,-\mathbf{p})
to ensure
\phi(t,\mathbf{x})\in\mathbb{R}
. We find that the Klein-Gordon equation is satisfied if
\partial_{t}^{2}\phi(t,\mathbf{p})+(m^{2}+\mathbf{p}^{2})\phi(t,\mathbf{p})=0
, which can be recognised as the equation of motion for a harmonic oscillator with frequency
\omega_{\mathbf{p}}=\sqrt{m^{2}+\mathbf{p}^{2}}
. Its solutions are
\phi(t,\mathbf{p})\propto\exp(\pm i\omega_{\mathbf{p}}t)
, and so any solution to (
B.6
) in Minkowski spacetime can, for some choice of coefficients
\phi_{\mathbf{p}}
, be expanded as

\displaystyle\phi(x)=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d-1}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{d-1}}\left(\phi_{\mathbf{p}}e^{ip\cdot x}+\phi_{\mathbf{p}}^{*}e^{-ip\cdot x}\right)\ ,

(B.8)

where
p\cdot x=p_{\mu}x^{\mu}
and
p^{0}=\omega_{\mathbf{p}}
. As a matter of aesthetics, one would like this expansion to be in terms of functions that are orthonormal in some appropriate sense. This appropriate sense is provided by the Klein-Gordon inner product, which for two functions
f_{1}(x)
and
f_{2}(x)
is
[
3
,
18
]

\displaystyle(f_{1},f_{2})=-i\int_{\Sigma}\left(f_{1}\nabla_{\mu}f_{2}^{*}-f_{2}^{*}\nabla_{\mu}f_{1}\right)n^{\mu}\sqrt{g_{\Sigma}}\,\mathrm{d}^{d-1}x\ ,

(B.9)

where
\sqrt{g_{\Sigma}}\,\mathrm{d}^{d-1}x
is the area element of a spacelike hypersurface
\Sigma
with unit normal vector
n^{\mu}
(i.e.
g_{\Sigma}
is the determinant of the restriction of the metric to
\Sigma
). From (
B.8
), we see that the basis functions in which
\phi(x)
is expanded are
f_{\mathbf{p}}(x)\propto e^{ip\cdot x}
; we fix the normalisation by demanding that
(f_{\mathbf{p}},f_{\mathbf{q}})=(2\pi)^{d-1}\delta(\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q})
if
\Sigma
is the hypersurface defined by
t=0
. With this normalisation, the expansion (
B.8
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\phi(x)=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d-1}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{d-1}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\omega_{\mathbf{p}}}}\left(a_{\mathbf{p}}e^{ip\cdot x}+a^{*}_{\mathbf{p}}e^{-ip\cdot x}\right)

(B.10)

for some coefficients
a_{\mathbf{p}}
. Finally, the conjugate momentum of
\phi
in Minkowski spacetime is
\pi(x)=\partial_{t}\phi(x)
.

Another important classical field theory is general relativity. In this theory, the metric
g_{ab}
is promoted to a dynamical field and assigned the Einstein-Hilbert action; writing
S_{\mathrm{mat}}
for the action of the relevant matter fields, the total action for general relativity is
[
2
]

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{gr}}=\frac{1}{16\pi G_{N}}\int R\,\sqrt{-g}\,\mathrm{d}^{d}x+S_{\mathrm{mat}}\ ,

(B.11)

where the first term on the right-hand side is the Einstein-Hilbert action. Applying the steps from (
B.1
)-(
B.3
) to
S_{\mathrm{gr}}
to obtain equations of motion for the metric is complicated by two factors: the Ricci scalar
R
is not constructed from covariant derivatives of
g_{ab}
and since we want to know the equations of motion for the metric, there should be a contribution from the variation of the volume element in (
B.2
). Nevertheless, one can vary the metric as
g_{ab}\rightarrow g_{ab}+\delta g_{ab}
; the resulting change in
S_{\mathrm{gr}}
is
[
3
]

\displaystyle\delta S_{\mathrm{gr}}=\frac{1}{16\pi G_{N}}\int\left(R_{ab}-\frac{1}{2}g_{ab}R\right)\delta g^{ab}\,\sqrt{-g}\,\mathrm{d}^{d}x+\delta S_{\mathrm{mat}}+\mathcal{O}\left((\delta g^{ab})^{2}\right)\ .

(B.12)

To simplify the notation, we introduce the functional derivative of
S
with respect to
g^{ab}
via

\displaystyle\delta S=\int\frac{\delta S}{\delta g^{ab}}\delta g^{ab}\,\mathrm{d}^{d}x+\mathcal{O}\left((\delta g^{ab})^{2}\right)\ .

(B.13)

Here,
\delta S
is the change in
S
due to a variation in the metric and
\frac{\delta S}{\delta g^{ab}}
is the functional derivative of
S
with respect to
g^{ab}
. With this definition, (
B.12
) can be written as

\displaystyle\delta S_{\mathrm{gr}}=\frac{1}{16\pi G_{N}}\int\left(R_{ab}-\frac{1}{2}g_{ab}R+\frac{16\pi G_{N}}{\sqrt{-g}}\frac{\delta S_{\mathrm{mat}}}{\delta g^{ab}}\right)\delta g^{ab}\,\sqrt{-g}\,\mathrm{d}^{d}x+\mathcal{O}\left((\delta g^{ab})^{2}\right)\ .

(B.14)

Because of the principle of least action, the configuration of the metric will be such that
\delta S_{\mathrm{gr}}=\mathcal{O}\left((\delta g^{ab})^{2}\right)
for any
\delta g^{ab}
; by (
B.14
), this implies that the metric should satisfy

\displaystyle R_{ab}-\frac{1}{2}g_{ab}R=-\frac{16\pi G_{N}}{\sqrt{-g}}\frac{\delta S_{\mathrm{mat}}}{\delta g^{ab}}\ .

(B.15)

By comparing this to the field equations of general relativity in (
1.1
), the energy-momentum tensor of the matter fields must be identified as

\displaystyle T_{ab}=-\frac{2}{\sqrt{-g}}\frac{\delta S_{\mathrm{mat}}}{\delta g^{ab}}\ .

(B.16)

This can be applied to the non-minimally coupled but otherwise free real scalar field from (
B.5
), leading to an energy-momentum tensor
[
72
]

\displaystyle T_{ab}=\nabla_{a}\phi\nabla_{b}\phi-\frac{1}{2}\left(\nabla_{c}\phi\nabla^{c}\phi+m^{2}\phi^{2}\right)g_{ab}+\xi\left(R_{ab}-\frac{1}{2}g_{ab}R+g_{ab}\nabla^{c}\nabla_{c}-\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\right)\phi^{2}\ .

(B.17)

B.2
Canonical quantisation

The previous section described several aspects of classical field theories; such classical theories can be developed into QFTs by a process known as ‘quantisation’. In this section, we discuss canonical quantisation, a rather simple way of going about this process, by quantising the free, real scalar field
\phi
(which we considered in the previous section) in Minkowski spacetime.

The essential idea of canonical quantisation is that we reinterpret the dynamical variables of a classical field theory as operators acting on a Hilbert space and obeying the canonical commutation relations. For example, non-relativistic quantum mechanics is usually concerned with systems that are classically described by a set of particles with generalised coordinates
q_{j}
and conjugate momenta
p_{j}
; these variables are promoted to operators
\hat{q}_{j}
and
\hat{p}_{j}
, which obey the canonical commutation relations
[\hat{q}_{j},\hat{p}_{k}]=i\delta_{jk}
and
[\hat{q}_{j},\hat{q}_{k}]=[\hat{p}_{j},\hat{p}_{k}]=0
. The analogous quantities for field theories in flat spacetime are the field
\Phi(t,\mathbf{x})
and its conjugate momentum
\Pi(t,\mathbf{y})
evaluated at the same time coordinate, which are promoted to operators
\hat{\Phi}(t,\mathbf{x})
and
\hat{\Pi}(t,\mathbf{y})
. If we take
\Phi
to be the real scalar field
\phi
, which was discussed in the previous section, then the generalisation of the non-relativistic canonical commutation relations is straightforward:

\displaystyle[\hat{\phi}(t,\mathbf{x}),\hat{\pi}(t,\mathbf{y})]=i\delta\left(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\right)\ ,

\displaystyle[\hat{\phi}(t,\mathbf{x}),\hat{\phi}(t,\mathbf{y})]=[\hat{\pi}(t,\mathbf{x}),\hat{\pi}(t,\mathbf{y})]=0\ .

(B.18)

Of course, promoting
\phi
and
\pi
to operators implies that the coefficients
a_{\mathbf{p}}
and
a_{\mathbf{p}}^{*}
from (
B.10
) are also promoted to operators, respectively
\hat{a}_{\mathbf{p}}
and
\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}}
. Using (
B.10
) and the fact that classically,
\pi(t,\mathbf{x})=\partial_{t}\phi(t,\mathbf{x})
, one can express
\hat{a}_{\mathbf{p}}
in terms of
\hat{\phi}
and
\hat{\pi}
:

\displaystyle\hat{a}_{\mathbf{p}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\omega_{\mathbf{p}}}}\int\left(i\hat{\pi}(x)+\omega_{\mathbf{p}}\hat{\phi}(x)\right)e^{-ip\cdot x}\mathrm{d}^{d-1}\mathbf{x}\ .

(B.19)

Then, using (
B.19
) in (
B.18
), the commutation relations for
\hat{a}_{\mathbf{p}}
and
\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}}
can be found:

\displaystyle[\hat{a}_{\mathbf{p}},\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q}}]=(2\pi)^{d-1}\delta(\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle[\hat{a}_{\mathbf{p}},\hat{a}_{\mathbf{q}}]=[\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}},\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q}}]=0\ .

(B.20)

To interpret the effect of acting with
\hat{a}_{\mathbf{p}}
and
\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}}
, note from (
B.19
) and (
B.20
) that they are the QFT analogues of the annihilation and creation operators of the quantum mechanical harmonic oscillator
[
47
]
. Thus, acting with
\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}}
on a state is interpreted as adding an excitation with momentum
\mathbf{p}
to the field, while acting with
\hat{a}_{\mathbf{p}}
is interpreted as removing that same excitation. This way,
\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}}
and
\hat{a}_{\mathbf{p}}
define particles: a particle with momentum
\mathbf{p}
is an excitation created by
\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}}
and annihilated by
\hat{a}_{\mathbf{p}}
.
\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}}
and
\hat{a}_{\mathbf{p}}
also define a vacuum state
\left|0\right\rangle
as the state without particles, i.e. such that
\hat{a}_{\mathbf{p}}\left|0\right\rangle=0
for all
\mathbf{p}
; by repeatedly acting on
\left|0\right\rangle
with
\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}}
for various
\mathbf{p}
, one obtains the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
of the theory. Physical observables are obtained as expectation values; for example, the expectation value of the field in a state
\left|\psi\right\rangle\in\mathcal{H}
is
\left\langle\psi\right|\hat{\phi}(x)\left|\psi\right\rangle=\langle\hat{\phi}(x)\rangle_{\psi}
.

Besides the field operators themselves, all other dynamical quantities from the classical theory are promoted to operators in the QFT. The usual strategy for this is to take the classical expression for some quantity in terms of
\phi(x)
and
\pi(x)
, and then replace
\phi\rightarrow\hat{\phi}
and
\pi\rightarrow\hat{\pi}
. However, if one would do this with e.g. the energy-momentum tensor in (
B.17
), one needs to understand how to handle products of operators like
\hat{\phi}(x)\hat{\phi}(x)
. The naive expression, obtained by simply multiplying the operator version of (
B.10
), is problematic:

\displaystyle\hat{\phi}(x)\hat{\phi}(x)=

\displaystyle\ \int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d-1}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{d-1}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d-1}\mathbf{q}}{(2\pi)^{d-1}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\omega_{\mathbf{p}}\omega_{\mathbf{q}}}}\left(\hat{a}_{\mathbf{p}}\hat{a}_{\mathbf{q}}e^{i(p+q)\cdot x}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}}\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q}}e^{-i(p+q)\cdot x}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\ \qquad\ \qquad\ \qquad\ \left.+\ \hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}}\hat{a}_{\mathbf{q}}e^{-i(p-q)\cdot x}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q}}\hat{a}_{\mathbf{p}}e^{i(p-q)\cdot x}\right)+\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d-1}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{d-1}}\frac{1}{2\omega_{\mathbf{p}}}\ .

(B.21)

The ordering of the creation and annihilation operators, with every creation operator to the left of the annihilation operators, is chosen so that the operator part of this expression has a vanishing expectation value in the vacuum state. However, this required commuting
\hat{a}_{\mathbf{p}}
and
\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q}}
, giving rise to the final term. This term is divergent, meaning that as an operator,
\hat{\phi}(x)\hat{\phi}(x)
does not make much sense: it gives an infinite expectation value in every state.

A resolution to this problem is provided by a procedure known as vacuum subtraction
[
77
]
. For this procedure, we first point-split the quantity we are interested in; in the present context, this would mean that we consider
\hat{\phi}(x)\hat{\phi}(y)
for
x\neq y
. Then, we subtract the expectation value of the resulting operator in the vacuum state to remove the offending term(s). Finally, we take the coincidence limit
y\rightarrow x
to obtain an operator that comes as close to the divergent original operator as possible. For
\hat{\phi}(x)\hat{\phi}(x)
, this procedure is carried out as follows:

\displaystyle:\!\hat{\phi}^{2}(x)\!:\ =

\displaystyle\ \lim_{y\rightarrow x}\left(\hat{\phi}(x)\hat{\phi}(y)-\left\langle 0\right|\hat{\phi}(x)\hat{\phi}(y)\left|0\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d-1}\mathbf{p}}{(2\pi)^{d-1}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d-1}\mathbf{q}}{(2\pi)^{d-1}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\omega_{\mathbf{p}}\omega_{\mathbf{q}}}}\left(\hat{a}_{\mathbf{p}}\hat{a}_{\mathbf{q}}e^{i(p+q)\cdot x}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}}\hat{a}_{\mathbf{q}}e^{-i(p-q)\cdot x}\right)+\mathrm{h.c.}\ ,

(B.22)

where the double colon indicates a vacuum subtracted operators and the abbreviation ‘h.c.’ denotes the Hermitian conjugate of the terms to its left. The point-splitting ensures that we are dealing with sensible (divergence-free) operators at every step; it is therefore a way of regulating the divergence in
\hat{\phi}(x)\hat{\phi}(x)
. Vacuum subtraction as a whole is an example of a renormalisation procedure, wherein one systematically removes (regulated) divergences.

B.3
Density matrices

The previous section demonstrated how a classical field theory can be used to construct a QFT via canonical quantisation. The resulting theory revolved around operators acting on states from the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, and extracted observables by taking expectation values. In this section, we consider an alternative way of obtaining observables: the density matrix
[
47
]
.

Consider a state
\left|\psi\right\rangle\in\mathcal{H}
; its density matrix is defined as
\rho^{\psi}=\left|\psi\right\rangle\left\langle\psi\right|
. Assuming that there exists a complete basis
1
1

1

For simplicity, this basis will be assumed to be discrete. In practice one would have to take a continuum limit, for example using a path integral.

\left\{\left|\psi_{i}\right\rangle\right\}
of
\mathcal{H}
, the expectation value of an operator
\hat{\mathcal{O}}
is

\displaystyle\left\langle\psi\right|\hat{\mathcal{O}}\left|\psi\right\rangle=\sum_{i}\left\langle\psi\right|\hat{\mathcal{O}}\left|\psi_{i}\right\rangle\left\langle\psi_{i}\,\right|\!\left.\psi\right\rangle=\sum_{i}\left\langle\psi_{i}\right|\rho\hat{\mathcal{O}}\left|\psi_{i}\right\rangle\equiv\operatorname{Tr}(\rho\hat{\mathcal{O}})\ ,

(B.23)

which also defined the trace operation. This can be generalised to mixed states, field configurations that cannot be described by an element of
\mathcal{H}
, by defining the density matrix as

\displaystyle\rho^{\psi}=\sum_{\alpha}c^{\psi}_{\alpha}\left|\psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle\left\langle\psi_{\alpha}\right|\ .

(B.24)

In this definition,
\left|\psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle\in\mathcal{H}
are unit vectors and the
c^{\psi}_{\alpha}\in[0,1]
are normalised such that
\sum_{\alpha}c^{\psi}_{\alpha}=1
. The expectation value of an operator is then defined analogously to (
B.23
), as

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\mathcal{O}}\rangle_{\psi}=\operatorname{Tr}(\rho^{\psi}\hat{\mathcal{O}})\ .

(B.25)

We may also consider a system for which the Hilbert space factorises, as
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}
. In that case, any state
\left|\psi\right\rangle\in\mathcal{H}
may be written as
\left|\psi\right\rangle=|\psi^{1}\rangle|\psi^{2}\rangle
, and the density matrix for subsystem 1 can be defined by taking a trace over subsystem 2 as follows:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}_{2}(\rho^{\psi})=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i}\langle\psi^{2}_{i}|\left(\sum_{\alpha}c^{\psi}_{\alpha}|\psi^{1}_{\alpha}\rangle|\psi^{2}_{\alpha}\rangle\langle\psi^{2}_{\alpha}|\langle\psi^{1}_{\alpha}|\right)|\psi^{2}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{\alpha}\sum_{i}\left|\langle\psi^{2}_{i}|\psi^{2}_{\alpha}\rangle\right|^{2}c^{\psi}_{\alpha}|\psi^{1}_{\alpha}\rangle\langle\psi^{1}_{\alpha}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{\alpha}c^{\psi}_{\alpha}|\psi^{1}_{\alpha}\rangle\langle\psi^{1}_{\alpha}|\equiv\rho^{\psi}_{1}\ ,

(B.26)

where we have chosen a basis
\left\{\left|\psi^{2}_{i}\right\rangle\right\}
for
\mathcal{H}_{2}
and assumed that
\left|\psi^{1}_{\alpha}\right\rangle\in\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\left|\psi^{2}_{\alpha}\right\rangle\in\mathcal{H}_{2}
are unit vectors. To confirm that
\rho^{\psi}_{1}
really acts like a density matrix, we can consider the expectation value of an operator
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}
which only acts on subsystem 1, which can be written as

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\rangle_{\psi}=

\displaystyle\ \operatorname{Tr}(\rho^{\psi}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i,j}\langle\psi^{2}_{i}|\langle\psi^{1}_{j}|\rho^{\psi}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}|\psi^{1}_{j}\rangle|\psi^{2}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{j}\langle\psi^{1}_{j}|\rho^{\psi}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}|\psi^{1}_{j}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \operatorname{Tr}_{1}(\rho^{\psi}_{1}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1})\ .

(B.27)

With the necessary replacements, this is indeed analogous to (
B.25
) when the trace and the density matrix are restricted to subsystem 1;
\rho^{\psi}_{1}
is therefore called the reduced density matrix.

B.4
Conformal field theory

The action as defined in section
B.1
is, by construction, invariant under general diffeomorphisms. However, inspired by section
A.4
one may ask how a Weyl transformation (
A.31
) would affect fields other than the metric, or equivalently, how one would relate a field theory in a spacetime
\tilde{M}
with metric
\tilde{g}_{ab}
to the same theory in a spacetime
M
with metric
g_{ab}=\Omega^{2}\tilde{g}_{ab}
. In this section, we briefly discuss two instances in which this question can be answered to some degree: a Weyl invariant classical field and conformally invariant quantum fields (CFTs). There are many much more pedagogical treatments of CFTs available, e.g.
[
78
,
79
]
.

B.4.1
Weyl invariance

To discuss Weyl invariance at the classical level, we must first understand how Weyl transformations affect classical fields. We start by realising that the action is dimensionless (we set
\hbar=c=1
). Since the volume element has mass dimension
-d
(i.e.
\sqrt{-g}\,\mathrm{d}^{d}x
has dimensions
(\text{mass})^{-d}
), the Lagrangian must have mass dimension
d
. From this restriction, the mass dimension
\Delta_{\Phi}
of any field
\Phi
can be found. If one then rescales all distances by a factor
\lambda
, the fields are naturally rescaled as

\displaystyle\Phi(x)\rightarrow\Phi^{\prime}(\lambda x)=\lambda^{-\Delta_{\Phi}}\Phi(x)\ .

(B.28)

A Weyl transformation
\tilde{g}_{ab}\rightarrow g_{ab}=\Omega^{2}\tilde{g}_{ab}
can be interpreted as a local rescaling of distances by a factor
\Omega(x)
. For sufficiently local fields, we therefore guess that they transform as

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}(x)\rightarrow\Phi(x)=\Omega^{-\Delta_{\tilde{\Phi}}}(x)\tilde{\Phi}(x)\ .

(B.29)

Fields which transform like this are called primary fields; a non-primary field is e.g.
\nabla_{a}\Phi
.

With the transformation (
B.29
), we can begin to understand how the dynamics of
\Phi
differ from those of
\tilde{\Phi}
. As an example, consider the non-minimally coupled real scalar
\phi
. From its Lagrangian (
B.5
) one finds that
\Delta_{\phi}=\frac{1}{2}d-1
, so a Weyl transformation
\tilde{g}_{ab}\rightarrow g_{ab}=\Omega^{2}\tilde{g}_{ab}
transforms the Lagrangian as

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{g}^{ab}\tilde{\nabla}_{a}\tilde{\phi}\tilde{\nabla}_{b}\tilde{\phi}-\frac{1}{2}\left(m^{2}+\xi\tilde{R}\right)\tilde{\phi}^{2}\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\ -\frac{1}{2}g^{ab}\nabla_{a}\phi\nabla_{b}\phi-\frac{1}{2}\left(m^{2}+\xi R\right)\phi^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ -\frac{1}{2}\Omega^{-d}\left[\tilde{g}^{ab}\tilde{\nabla}_{a}\tilde{\phi}\tilde{\nabla}_{b}\tilde{\phi}+\left(m^{2}\Omega^{2}+\xi\tilde{R}\right)\tilde{\phi}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\ -\frac{d-1}{2\Omega^{d}}\left(\frac{d-2}{4(d-1)}-\xi\right)\tilde{\phi}^{2}\tilde{g}^{ab}\left(2\tilde{\nabla}_{a}\tilde{\nabla}_{b}\ln\Omega\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\ \qquad\ \qquad\ \quad\ \left.+\ (d-2)\tilde{\nabla}_{a}\ln\Omega\tilde{\nabla}_{b}\ln\Omega\right)\ ,

(B.30)

where we have used (
A.41
) and (
B.29
). From (
B.30
), one can observe that for the particular choice of parameters
m=0
and
\xi=\frac{1}{4}(d-2)/(d-1)
, the Lagrangian transforms as
\mathcal{L}\rightarrow\Omega^{-d}\mathcal{L}
. By the definition (
B.1
), this in turn implies that for
m=0
and
\xi=\frac{1}{4}(d-2)/(d-1)
, we have

\displaystyle S[\phi,g_{ab}]=S[\tilde{\phi},\tilde{g}_{ab}]

(B.31)

with
\phi=\Omega^{1-d/2}\tilde{\phi}
and
g_{ab}=\Omega^{2}\tilde{g}_{ab}
; any field theory which obeys (
B.31
) is called Weyl invariant. A useful property of Weyl invariant theories is that the energy-momentum tensors
\tilde{T}_{ab}
and
T_{ab}
of the theory in
\tilde{M}
and
M
can be related, which can be seen by realising that a variation
\delta\tilde{g}^{ab}
induces a variation
\delta g^{ab}=\Omega^{-2}\delta\tilde{g}^{ab}
. Then, by (
B.16
), we have

\displaystyle T_{ab}=-\frac{2}{\sqrt{-g}}\frac{\delta S[\Phi,g_{ab}]}{\delta g^{ab}}=-\frac{2}{\Omega^{d}\sqrt{-\tilde{g}}}\frac{\delta S[\tilde{\Phi},\tilde{g}_{ab}]}{\Omega^{-2}\delta\tilde{g}^{ab}}=\Omega^{2-d}\tilde{T}_{ab}\ ,

(B.32)

where we generalised to any Weyl invariant field theory. Another useful property is that the energy-momentum tensor is traceless, which follows from performing an infinitesimal Weyl transformation. In effect, this varies the metric by
\delta g^{ab}=\sigma(x)g^{ab}
, which in turn changes
S
by

\displaystyle\delta S=\int\frac{\delta S}{\delta g^{ab}}\delta g^{ab}\mathrm{d}^{d}x+\mathcal{O}\left((\delta g^{ab})^{2}\right)=-\frac{1}{2}\int\sigma(x)T_{ab}g^{ab}\,\sqrt{-g}\,\mathrm{d}^{d}x+\mathcal{O}(\sigma^{2})\ .

(B.33)

If the theory is Weyl invariant, then any infinitesimal Weyl transformation should lead to
\delta S=0
; by (
B.33
), this implies that
g^{ab}T_{ab}=0
.

For QFTs, the effect of Weyl transformations is more involved. For one, quantising a Weyl invariant classical field theory does not necessarily lead to a Weyl invariant QFT; in fact, the invariance is usually broken by quantum effects in interactions. Even in the case that one has a Weyl invariant QFT, not all results from the classical theory carry over to the QFT. Although the transformation (
B.29
) can be reinterpreted as a statement about primary operators, the transformation (
B.32
) of the energy-momentum tensor must in general be modified to

\displaystyle U\tilde{T}_{ab}U^{\dagger}=\Omega^{d-2}\left(T_{ab}-X_{ab}\right)\ ,

(B.34)

where
U:\tilde{\mathcal{H}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is a unitary map from the Hilbert space
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}
of the QFT in a geometry described by
\tilde{g}_{ab}
to the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
of the same QFT in a geometry described by
g_{ab}=\Omega^{2}\tilde{g}_{ab}
;
X_{ab}
is called the conformal or Weyl anomaly. It is completely determined by the geometry, vanishes for odd
d
, and arises due to the need to introduce geometric counterterms that render the action of a QFT finite
[
51
,
56
,
71
]
; from the point of view of vacuum subtraction, one might say that the vacuum expectation value which should be subtracted from the energy-momentum tensor depends on the geometry in a way that is captured by
X_{ab}

[
77
,
80
]
. Similarly, renormalisation in curved spacetime leads to the violation of the classical identity
g^{ab}T_{ab}=0
; instead, the trace of the energy-momentum tensor becomes equal to a local geometric quantity which vanishes in Minkowski spacetime
[
77
]
.

B.4.2
Conformal invariance

Closely related to Weyl invariance is the concept of conformal invariance. A conformally invariant field theory is not demanded to be invariant under general Weyl transformations but only under those Weyl transformations that leave the metric invariant up to a diffeomorphism, i.e. such that

\displaystyle g_{\mu\nu}(x)=\Omega^{2}(x)\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}(x)=\frac{\partial\tilde{x}^{\rho}}{\partial x^{\mu}}\frac{\partial\tilde{x}^{\sigma}}{\partial x^{\nu}}\tilde{g}_{\rho\sigma}(\tilde{x})\ .

(B.35)

The combination of such a Weyl transformation with a diffeomorphism that removes the factor
\Omega^{2}
is called a conformal transformation
2
2

2

Some authors reserve the term ‘conformal transformation’ for the Weyl transformation
[
50
]
, while others apply it only to the diffeomorphism
[
78
]
.
; many spacetimes do not allow conformal transformations, because there are no diffeomorphisms that only rescale their metric. However, they are allowed in Minkowski spacetime. Specifically, Lorentz boosts and translations are conformal transformations when combined with a trivial Weyl transformation (
\Omega=1
), as are rigid scale transformations (dilatations) when they follow a uniform Weyl transformation, with
\Omega=\lambda
a constant. Finally, one may do a Weyl transformation with
\Omega^{2}=(1-2b\cdot x+b^{2}x^{2})^{-2}
for some fixed vector
b^{a}
and
x^{2}=\eta_{\mu\nu}x^{\mu}x^{\nu}
; one obtains a conformal transformation by following this Weyl transformation with a diffeomorphism which sends

\displaystyle x^{\mu}\rightarrow x^{\prime\mu}=\frac{x^{\mu}-b^{\mu}x^{2}}{1-b_{\nu}x^{\nu}+b^{2}x^{2}}\ .

(B.36)

These transformations (boosts, translations, dilatations and special conformal transformations) leave the Minkowski metric invariant, making them highly relevant to QFTs in flat spacetime. If the dynamics of a QFT are invariant under conformal transformations, the QFT is called a conformal field theory (CFT); an explicit example of a CFT is the real scalar described by (
B.5
) for
m=0
and
\xi=\frac{1}{4}(d-2)/(d-1)
. This is the reason that this value of
\xi
is also called conformal coupling.

CFTs are closely related to Weyl invariant theories since conformal transformations are based on a subset of all possible Weyl transformations. This means that our discussion of Weyl invariant theories also applies to CFTs; in particular, (
B.29
) and (
B.34
) continue to describe the transformation of respectively primary operators and the energy-momentum tensor under the Weyl transformation involved in a conformal transformation (the diffeomorphism may introduce an additional transformation). In fact, the connection between CFTs and Weyl invariant QFTs goes even deeper: it can be shown that field theories that are conformally invariant in Minkowski spacetime are also Weyl invariant in general spacetimes
[
50
]
.
